Reverse ETL is Eating the Pipeline\nThe hot trend isn't just getting data into warehouses anymore. The UX of analytics is being rewritten from the ground up.\n\n4. The Conversational Dashboard\nNot dead, but evolving fast. Dashboards are becoming dialogue. Ask your data a question in natural language, get a visualization back. But here's the catch — this only works with a governed semantic layer underneath. Without it, you get hallucinations, not insights.\n\n→ The companies that will win in 2025-2026 are the ones treating their data platform as a product, not a pipeline.\n\n→ They're thinking about developer experience for their internal data consumers — analysts, product managers, ops teams.\n\n→ They're investing in metadata management before adding another AI chatbot.\n\nThe real industry trend isn't any single technology. It's the convergence of AI interaction patterns with solid data architecture fundamentals. The hype cycle is separating the signal from the noise.\n\nWhat's one industry shift you're watching that's about to break wide open? The Rise of Agentic Data Pipelines\n\nTeams are moving from \"build a dashboard and hope someone looks at it\" to \"build an autonomous agent that monitors, detects, and acts on anomalies in real time.\" One team I spoke with reduced their incident response time from 4 hours to 12 minutes using this approach.\n\n2. The Death of the All-Purpose Data Platform\n\nThe trend is toward specialized, composable architectures. Best-of-breed tools connected by a unified metadata layer. Not one monolithic platform that does everything poorly.\n\nThe companies winning right now aren't the ones with the most data. They're the ones whose data architecture is designed for AI consumption.\n\nThe gap between \"we have a data stack\" and \"our data stack is AI-ready\" is wider than most teams realize.\n\nIf you're a data leader, ask yourself this week: Is your architecture built for humans looking at screens or for agents taking action? Because the answer determines whether you'll be playing catch-up or leading the next wave.\n\nWhat shifts are you seeing in data architecture right now? DEFINE THE CONTRACT FIRST\nBefore building a single pipeline, teams are now writing a formal agreement between producer and consumer. What schema is expected? What freshness SLA? VERSION EVERYTHING LIKE AN API\nWhen a producer changes a column type, it's a breaking change — just like an API. Teams are adopting semantic versioning for datasets with proper deprecation windows and migration guides for consumers.\n\n4. MEASURE CONTRACT COMPLIANCE\nThe metric that matters: percentage of data assets with active contracts. The best teams track this like uptime. Target is 95%+ coverage across all production datasets.\n\nWhy this matters now: as data mesh and data product thinking scale, contracts become the glue that makes decentralization work without chaos. Data contracts are becoming standard\n\nThe wild west of \"just pipe the raw table and hope for the best\" is ending. But by the time they flag it, the dashboard's already been shared, the exec already made the call, and the ML model already drifted.\n\nEnter data observability — and it's becoming the fastest-growing category in the data stack for a reason.\n\n→ First-gen tools told you what happened (descriptive)\n→ Second-gen told you why it happened (diagnostic)\n→ Third-gen tells you what will break before it breaks (predictive)\n\nThe shift I'm tracking:\n\n1. Freshness as a service-level objective\nCompanies are moving from \"we check nightly\" to real-time freshness SLAs. If your customer-facing dashboard is more than 5 minutes stale, that's an incident — not a \"we'll fix it tomorrow.\"\n\n2. The death of siloed monitoring\nData teams, engineering teams, and business teams used separate tools. Now unified observability platforms are collapsing those boundaries — because a dbt model failure in staging IS a revenue problem in production.\n\n3. Schema drift is the new silent killer\nMonte Carlo reports that 60%+ of data incidents stem from schema changes nobody caught. AI-generated pipelines make this worse — models change output schemas silently, and your downstream dashboards just break without alerting.\n\nThe companies winning at data right now aren't the ones with the fanciest ML. They're the ones who know, in real time, that their data is trustworthy.\n\nBecause trust is the real currency. And you can't buy it back after a broken report goes viral in the C-suite.\n\nWhat's your data observability stack looking like? The same theme kept surfacing:\n\nTheir teams build beautiful dashboards. Executives click once, say \"cool\", and never return.\n\nThe real value isn't in visualization — it's in action. And action requires AI agents that can reason over your data, not just display it.\n\nHere's what's actually shifting in 2025:\n\n1. Agent-native data layers are replacing traditional semantic layers. Companies are embedding their business logic directly into LLM-callable APIs instead of relying on humans to interpret charts.\n\n2. The \"query middleman\" is dying. Analysts used to translate business questions into SQL, then translate results back. Now AI agents are doing both — and the bottleneck becomes data quality, not query skill.\n\n3. Real-time triggers are overtaking scheduled reports. Why wait for a Monday morning email when an agent can detect the anomaly at 2:47 PM on a Wednesday and act on it?\n\nThe teams winning this transition share one trait: they stopped treating data as something to be looked at and started treating it as something to be acted upon.\n\nThey're investing in data contracts, semantic tagging, and agent-friendly APIs. Not prettier dashboards.\n\nThe companies that figure this out will pull ahead fast. The ones doubling down on visualization alone? They'll be producing reports for an audience that's no longer watching.\n\nWhat's your take — are you seeing your data team shift toward agent-native architecture, or is it still dashboard-heavy? Domain ownership, not domain isolation — teams own their data products but share a common ingestion layer and governance framework\n2. Central platforms, decentralized execution — the platform team builds the highway; business teams drive their own cars\n3. Governance as enabler, not gatekeeper — automated quality checks replace manual approval bottlenecks\n4. Metrics as contracts — cross-domain SLAs on data freshness and accuracy replace endless meetings\n\nThe companies winning with data are not the ones with the purest architecture. They are the ones pragmatic enough to blend central platform efficiency with domain-level speed.\n\nWhat is your team's data architecture approach? Centralized, federated, or something in between? I'm seeing forward-thinking teams adopt streaming architectures (Kafka, Flink, RisingWave) as their primary data layer.\n\n3. Governance Becomes Code\nWhen agents act autonomously, static RBAC isn't enough. You need data contracts — machine-readable policies that define what, when, and how agents can use data. Think of them as runtime guardrails.\n\n4. Observability Gets a Third Dimension\nIt used to be: data quality + pipeline monitoring. Now add agent behavior tracking. What decision did the agent make? What data drove it? Was it logged? Companies without this visibility will face compliance nightmares.\n\n→ The real competitive advantage right now isn't having the best AI models.\n→ It's having the cleanest, most accessible, and well-governed data feeding those models.\n\nYour data infrastructure is your moat. Agents execute recommendations — adjusting pricing, rerouting inventory, optimizing campaigns — all without a human clicking 'approve.' The pipeline from data to decision just collapsed.\n\n2. I'm seeing forward-thinking teams adopt streaming architectures (Kafka, Flink, RisingWave) as their primary data layer.\n\n3. Governance Becomes Code\nWhen agents act autonomously, static RBAC isn't enough. You need data contracts — machine-readable policies that define what, when, and how agents can use data. Think of them as runtime guardrails.\n\n4. Observability Gets a Third Dimension\nIt used to be: data quality + pipeline monitoring. Now add agent behavior tracking. What decision did the agent make? What data drove it? Was it logged? Companies without this visibility will face compliance nightmares.\n\n→ The real competitive advantage right now isn't having the best AI models.\n→ It's having the cleanest, most accessible, and well-governed data feeding those models.\n\nYour data infrastructure is your moat. Protect it.\n\nWhat's the biggest data challenge your team is facing with AI adoption? Each team defines \"revenue\" differently — and execs get three different answers in every meeting.\n\n2. The semantic layer gap. Modern data stacks gave us incredible compute power — Snowflake, Databricks, BigQuery can query anything in seconds. But they left a void between raw tables and business meaning. When you can't map a column name to a business concept, every dashboard becomes an interpretation.\n\n3. Self-serve analytics became self-harm. We gave teams dashboard access without data literacy guardrails. The result? Cherry-picked metrics presented as objective truth. Confirmation bias, now with real-time charts.\n\n→ The smartest orgs are investing in semantic layers — dbt Metrics, Cube, LookML — to define business logic once and enforce it consistently.\n\n→ They're using AI not to generate flashy visuals, but to answer one question instantly: \"Where did this number come from?\"\n\n→ They're treating analytics like a product: version-controlled, documented, with SLAs on data freshness.\n\nThe companies that crack data trust first will make faster, better decisions through this entire economic cycle.\n\nWhat's your biggest data trust challenge right now?\n\n👇","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

Now the market is demanding we treat it as the product itself.\n\nHere's the shift I'm seeing play out in real-time.\n\n→ Traditional approach: Build a pipeline, dump in warehouse, hope someone queries it\n→ Emerging approach: Design a data product with SLAs, schemas, and customers\n\nThe difference is profound.\n\nI've been watching how forward-thinking companies are structuring this:\n\n1. Define your data asset like a software product\n   Every dataset gets an owner, a documented schema, a version, and observable SLAs. If your dbt model doesn't have a freshness check and a defined consumer, it's not a product yet.\n\n2. Chargeback and value tracking\n   Teams using data products internally now track cost per query, compute consumption per domain, and business impact per table. Data becomes a P&L line item, not a cost center.\n\n3. The rise of the data marketplace\n   Internal data catalogs are evolving into true marketplaces with search, rating, and discovery. Snowflake Marketplace, Databricks Unity Catalog, even internal mesh implementations. The ones that work? They treat data like products on a digital shelf.\n\n4. Contracts between domains\n   Data contracts (schema + SLAs + semantic meaning) between source and consumer teams. If the source changes the schema, the contract breaks — not the downstream pipeline. This is the single biggest unlock for data mesh at scale.\n\nWhat I find interesting is that the teams succeeding here aren't the ones with the most complex architectures.\n\nThey're the ones who stopped asking \"what data can we collect?\" and started asking \"what data product are we building and who's it for?\"\n\nThat one question changes everything about how you design, document, and deliver.\n\nIs your org treating data like a product yet? Or still chasing pipelines?\n\nDrop your biggest challenge with data product thinking below. 👇","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

It's a mindset change.\n\nTeams are moving away from monolithic data platforms toward composable, agent-driven architectures. Here's what that actually looks like in practice:\n\n1. Pipelines are becoming event-aware\nInstead of batch jobs that run on a schedule, teams are deploying lightweight inference layers between their extraction and transformation stages. The pipeline doesn't just move data — it interprets it.\n\n2. Governance is shifting left\nThe hottest trend isn't a new catalog tool. It's embedding quality checks directly into the ingestion layer. Teams are catching schema drift, PII leaks, and distribution shifts before data ever hits the warehouse. The cost of fixing at ingest vs. fix at analytics? Roughly 12x difference.\n\n3. The rise of the hybrid analyst\nCompanies are asking analysts to own lightweight model deployment alongside their SQL work. Not to replace data scientists — but to close the gap between insight and action. The analyst who can write a prompt, evaluate output, and ship a small agent loop? That's the 2025 unicorn.\n\n4. Real-time is becoming the default expectation\nNot milliseconds for everything — but daily batch is starting to feel like dial-up. Teams are adopting micro-batch windows (60-90 seconds) for 80% of their tables. The tooling is finally mature enough that this doesn't require a dedicated streaming infrastructure team.\n\nWhat I find most interesting? None of these are about a specific tool.\n\nThey're about a shift in how we think about the data lifecycle. From passive storage to active intelligence.\n\nIf you're rebuilding your architecture this year, what's the one change that's made the biggest difference?\n\nDrop your take below. DATA CONTRACTS -> The governance trend quietly taking over. Teams write machine-readable agreements between data producers and consumers. When a schema changes, the contract breaks immediately - no more silent pipeline failures at 3 AM.\n\nThe companies winning in 2025 are not the ones collecting the most data. They are the ones with the thinnest layer between their data and their decisions.\n\nWhich of these trends are you seeing play out in your world? The gap between what leaders THINK their data infrastructure looks like and what it actually is? Operational analytics, not just dashboards — The winners embed insights directly into workflows. A dashboard you check once a week is a museum piece, not a decision tool.\n\n2. The era of data for data's sake is over.\n\nWhat changed? In 2023, data leaders asked me \"what's possible?\" In 2025, they ask \"what's the cheapest way to make this possible?\"\n\nThe smartest take I've heard recently: \"Build your data stack like you're paying for it out of your own pocket.\"\n\nAgree? MONETIZE THE ASSET — I'm seeing more orgs package their proprietary data into revenue-generating products. The question isn't \"how much data can we store?\" anymore.\n\nIt's \"what can our data DO that nothing else can?\"\n\nThe companies that figure that out first will define the next decade of their markets.\n\nAre you building infrastructure — or are you building products?\n\nDrop your biggest data stack frustration below. 👇","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

Something more nuanced.\n\n→ Companies are shifting from dashboards to autonomous data agents\n→ Instead of humans querying databases, databases are querying humans\n→ The paradigm flips: pull → push\n\nHere's what I'm seeing in the wild:\n\n1. Agentic data layers — Startups building middleware that lets LLMs talk directly to warehouse data via semantic layers. Not RAG over docs, but RAG over live schemas.\n\n2. Natural language governance — The old guard says \"just give analysts SQL access.\" The new wave says \"let the AI write the SQL, humans just approve.\" Governance becomes a review layer, not a gate.\n\n3. Embedded analytics is dying — Sending users to a BI dashboard is last decade. The trend is bringing insights to where decisions happen: Slack, Teams, CRM, email. Context-switching kills action.\n\n4. The rise of the \"Data Product Manager\" — A new role emerging at the intersection of product thinking and data architecture. Someone who treats datasets like products with SLAs, docs, and customers.\n\nThe bottom line?\n\nThe teams that win the next 18 months won't be the ones with the best models. They'll be the ones with the cleanest semantic layers and the fastest feedback loops between data producers and consumers.\n\nYour data stack is only as good as the decisions it drives. Period.\n\nWhat trend are you watching that nobody's talking about yet? Here's what changed.\n\n→ Two years ago, data engineering was about ETL pipelines and BI dashboards\n→ Today, it's about feeding AI models with reliable, real-time data\n→ The tools haven't changed as much as the expectations have\n\nI was on a call with a CTO last week who said something that stuck with me. He told me his team spends 60% of their time on data infrastructure for AI use cases. Not reporting. Not analytics. AI.\n\nHere are the 3 shifts I'm seeing across the industry right now:\n\n1. Feature stores are quietly replacing data marts. Teams are optimizing pipelines for ML training cycles, not monthly board reports. If your data platform isn't built for feature engineering, you're already behind.\n\n2. Data quality now has a direct line to model output. A schema change in your source system doesn't just break a dashboard anymore — it causes your RAG pipeline to serve hallucinated responses to customers. The blast radius is bigger than ever.\n\n3. Streaming-first is becoming the default. Batch pipelines worked when daily reports were sufficient. But real-time personalization, fraud detection, and AI agents demand sub-second data freshness. Organizations are retrofitting streaming into architectures that were never designed for it.\n\nThe most successful teams right now are the ones that stopped drawing a line between data engineering and ML engineering. They share roadmaps. They share tooling decisions. And they treat data quality as a model performance issue, not a reporting issue.\n\nThis is the biggest shift in data work I've seen since the move to cloud data warehouses.\n\nWhat about you — is your team merging data and ML pipelines or keeping them separate? There’s no longer one “modern data stack” — there are two diverging paths.\n\nHere’s what emerged:\n\n1. The Speed Track (startups & scale-ups): DuckDB + dbt + lightweight ingestion + a simple serving layer. Total monthly cost under $500. No dedicated warehouse. No orchestrator. Just SQL on object storage and fast iteration.\n\n2. The Scale Track (enterprises): Snowflake/Databricks + Unity Catalog + Airflow/Dagster + Fivetran + dbt + enterprise BI. $100k+/month. Full governance. Row-level security. 500+ daily active consumers.\n\nWhat’s vanishing?\n\nThe middle. Teams spending $5k-$20k/month on a single cloud warehouse with 10-20 users. They’re either scaling up or stripping down.\n\n→ Teams optimizing for speed are abandoning always-on warehouses for serverless compute.\n→ Teams requiring governance are doubling down on catalogs, RBAC, and audit trails.\n\nThe pivot point? Around 50 data consumers. Below that, the light stack wins on cost and velocity. Above that, the heavy stack becomes unavoidable.\n\nThe most dangerous place to be: stuck between both — paying enterprise prices for startup-level governance.\n\nIf your warehouse bill is growing without proportional velocity gains, it’s time to pick a direction.\n\nWhich track is your team on — Speed or Scale? 👇","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

Full governance. Row-level security. 500+ daily active consumers.\n\nWhat’s vanishing?\n\nThe middle. Teams spending $5k-$20k/month on a single cloud warehouse with 10-20 users. They’re either scaling up or stripping down.\n\n→ Teams optimizing for speed are abandoning always-on warehouses for serverless compute.\n→ Teams requiring governance are doubling down on catalogs, RBAC, and audit trails.\n\nThe pivot point? Around 50 data consumers. Below that, the light stack wins on cost and velocity. Above that, the heavy stack becomes unavoidable.\n\nThe most dangerous place to be: stuck between both — paying enterprise prices for startup-level governance.\n\nIf your warehouse bill is growing without proportional velocity gains, it’s time to pick a direction.\n\nWhich track is your team on — Speed or Scale? 👇","visibility":"PUBLIC","distribution":{"feedDistribution":"MAIN_FEED","targetEntities":[],"thirdPartyDistributionChannels":[]},"lifecycleState":"PUBLISHED","isReshareDisabledByAuthor":false}

Snowflake's Cortex AI, Databricks' AI/BI, and open-source projects like Superagent are all converging on the same thesis: data pipelines should reason, not just run.\n\nThe companies winning this year won't be the ones with the most complex dbt models. They'll be the ones whose data infrastructure can adapt faster than their competitors' business models change.\n\nOne question keeping me up at night: if agents can build and fix pipelines autonomously, what becomes of the data engineer's role?\n\nMy take — the title changes from \"pipeline builder\" to \"agent supervisor + quality gatekeeper.\" The work gets more strategic, less operational.\n\nWhat's your prediction?
